From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.3, F33.3, F20.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in—thanks for coming today. Can you tell me what's been bringing you in to see me?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I'm here because... it's just, it's a lot that's been going on at work lately, and it's getting to the point where I can't just ignore it anymore. I drive a bus, you know, for the city, and lately—well, not just lately, it's been building up for a while now—dispatch and compliance, they've been tracking my route changes. And I know everyone says, \"Oh, they track all the buses, it's just GPS, it's just normal oversight,\" but it's not just that. It's the way they do it. It's through the computer system, obviously, but then they make these... these little comments on the radio. Just tiny things that sound like regular dispatch talk to anyone else, but I know what they mean. They're letting me know they're watching my specific movements. And it's not just management either. I can tell certain other drivers are reporting on me. Like, they'll pull up at a layover or pass me on a route, and there's this look, or they'll key their mic right after I do. It's a whole system they've got set up just to monitor me, and people keep telling me it's just standard procedure, but I know the difference between standard procedure and being targeted. I really do. It's exhausting trying to drive safely when you know everyone is coordinating against you like that.\n\nDoctor: How long has this feeling of being specifically watched and targeted at work been going on—days, weeks, or months?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit down and trace it back to when I knew for absolutely sure, it hasn't just been a few days, I can tell you that. It's been... I'd say it's been about two months now where it's been undeniably clear to me. Before that, maybe there were little things, you know, things you could brush off as a coincidence if you were trying really hard to ignore it. But about two months ago, that was when the tracking through the computer system really locked in, and the radio comments started matching up perfectly with my exact movements. I remember it was right around the time they did a software update on the consoles in the cabs, and suddenly dispatch was making these highly specific remarks on the radio right as I was making turns or adjusting for traffic. That's when I knew it wasn't just my imagination. So for the past two months, every single shift, I log in and I can just feel the shift in how they monitor me. The radio clicks, the specific tone they use when they ask for my location even though the computer already tells them exactly where my bus is... it's been a solid eight weeks of this. Two whole months of just this constant, heavy feeling of being watched and managed in this very specific, targeted way, day in and day out. You have no idea how draining it is to keep up a professional face for two months when you know exactly what they're doing on the other end of that radio.\n\nDoctor: Besides the radio comments and mic clicks that you interpret as targeted messages, have you ever heard voices, sounds, or other things that other people around you couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's nothing like that. I want to be really clear about that, because I know how this all sounds, but I'm not... I'm not hearing things that aren't there. The things I'm talking about, the radio clicks, the dispatchers, the comments from other drivers—those are physical sounds coming out of a physical speaker in the cab. Anyone on my bus can hear them. If a passenger is sitting in the priority seating right behind my partition, they hear the exact same voice from compliance that I do. They just don't understand the context, you know? They just hear the static and think it's, 'Oh, Bus 42, check your headway,' or whatever standard phrase dispatch is using to mask it that day. \n\nBut as far as, like, voices whispering in my ear or sounds in an empty room that literally no one else can physically hear? No, absolutely not. I mean, my work environment is incredibly loud to begin with. You've got the engine, the air brakes hissing, the doors opening and closing every two blocks, people talking loudly on their phones... so there's a lot of noise all the time. But my hearing is perfectly fine. I actually have to get my hearing checked for my DOT physical—the Department of Transportation requires a full medical clearance every two years to keep your commercial driver's license—and I always pass the hearing test with flying colors. They put you in the little booth with the headphones and you press the button when you hear the beep, and I never miss one. \n\nSo I hear exactly what exists in the real world. I'm not making up phantom voices or hearing ghosts or anything like that. It's just the real, actual voices on the radio that are the problem, because of what they're coordinating and how they're tracking me. So, no, I don't hear things other people can't hear. They hear the exact same audio I do, they just don't know what it actually means.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this situation at work, have you noticed any other unusual thoughts, beliefs, or changes in how you think or express yourself—things like difficulty organizing your thoughts, pulling away from friends or family, or feeling less motivated or emotionally flat in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... it's hard to separate it all out, honestly, because the work thing takes up so much of my mental space. But if I really think about it, yeah, things outside of work have definitely changed. It's like... I just don't have the energy for the things I used to do. It's not just being tired from driving, it's this whole other kind of exhaustion. \n\nLike, I used to sing with this community choir on Tuesday nights. I've been doing it for three years, and I loved it. We were working on this really beautiful arrangement of a folk song, and I had a solo part coming up, but lately... I just stopped going. I can't even explain why, exactly. I just look at the clock on Tuesday evening and the thought of getting back in my car, driving to the community center, standing there and trying to project my voice and smile and interact with everyone... it just feels impossible. It feels like there's a heavy blanket over me. And the same goes for my softball team. I play in a rec league on the weekends, and I used to always be the one who volunteered to host the team at my place after a game. I'd make a big batch of chili or order pizzas, and we'd all hang out on my patio. But I haven't done that in weeks. I haven't even been going to the games. I just text the captain and say I'm not feeling well, but the truth is, I just don't want to be around them. I don't want to talk. I don't feel like I have anything to say anymore. \n\nAnd it's not just the social stuff. It's just... existing at home is hard now. Most days, after my shift—after dealing with the radio and the tracking and all of that—I come home, I lock the door, and I just sit down at the kitchen table. I leave my planner open in front of me, thinking I need to figure out what I'm doing the rest of the week, or I need to write down a grocery list, but I just sit there. I stare at the pages. I know I need to make dinner. I know I should probably call my mom back, because she left a voicemail two days ago, or I should pay the electric bill, but I just can't make myself start. It's like the connection between knowing I need to do something and actually physically moving my body to do it is completely broken. I just sit there for hours sometimes, perfectly still, feeling completely blank. It's not that I'm sad, exactly. It's more like I'm just... empty. Like someone pulled the plug and all the motivation just drained out of me. So, yeah, I guess you could say things have changed outside of work, too. It's just really hard to care about anything right now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this kind of emptiness, low motivation, and pulling away from choir and softball—did it start around the same time as the tracking concerns two months ago, or did it begin separately?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask if they started at the same time, because I actually tried to map it all out in my head recently. I was sitting there at my kitchen table, just staring at my planner like I told you, and I was trying to figure out when exactly I stopped caring about things. And no, it didn't start two months ago with the radio stuff. It's actually been going on a lot longer than that. \n\nIf I'm being completely honest, this feeling... this heavy, empty feeling... it's been building for about eight months now. Yeah, it's been a solid eight months. It started right around the time management put me in this temporary dispatch lead role at the depot. One of the regular supervisors had to go on an extended medical leave—I think it was for a knee replacement or something, he was always complaining about the stairs up to the breakroom—so they pulled me off my regular driving route to fill in for him. At the time, I thought it was going to be a really good opportunity, you know? A chance to sit in the office, learn the back-end of the routing system, see how everything is managed from the inside instead of just being out on the road all day. \n\nBut it was just... I don't know, something about it just started draining the life out of me. Sitting in that room, staring at the screens, dealing with the schedules... that's when I first noticed I didn't want to go to softball practice anymore. I'd finish my shift in the dispatch center, and I wouldn't even be physically tired because I'd just been sitting in a chair all day, but I'd feel so incredibly hollow. I just couldn't bring myself to go socialize. And then, even after the temporary role ended and they put me back behind the wheel on my normal route—which honestly should have been a relief—the feeling never went away. It just kept getting heavier and heavier. \n\nSo the tracking, the computer updates, the specific comments on the radio... all of that got undeniably real and locked in two months ago. But this feeling of being completely unplugged from my own life, the pulling away from choir and my friends? That's been creeping up on me for eight months, ever since I took that temporary dispatch job. It's just been this slow, steady fade out, month after month, until I ended up where I am now, just sitting in my house not able to move.\n\nDoctor: In the past two months, have there been any days or stretches of time when you felt your usual energy and mood were okay—not empty or hopeless—but you still strongly believed dispatch and other drivers were tracking and targeting you?\n\nPatient: No, I... I can't really think of a single day like that. It's not like I wake up on a Tuesday and think, oh, the sun is shining, I feel great, I have all this energy to go for a run or do my laundry, but then I get to work and realize they're still tracking me. It just doesn't work like that at all. \n\nEvery single day for the past two months, it's just been this relentless, heavy combination of both things at the exact same time. Like, take last weekend for example. I had Sunday off, and usually, back in the day, a Sunday off meant I was up early, making a big breakfast, maybe going to the farmer's market downtown before it got too crowded. But this past Sunday, I woke up at 7:00 AM—which is my normal time because my body clock is just so permanently locked into the early depot shift—and I just lay there in bed for hours. I was staring up at the ceiling fan. The blades have this little wobble to them on the medium setting, and I was just watching it go round and round, feeling completely drained. Like my arms and legs were literally made of wet cement. I didn't care about breakfast, I didn't care about the daylight outside, I just felt that massive, hollow emptiness I was telling you about. \n\nAnd even then, while I'm lying there feeling totally flat and unable to even push the blankets off me, my brain is still completely locked onto what dispatch is doing. I'm lying there in the quiet, but I'm thinking about how they updated the routing software on Friday afternoon right before my shift ended, and how they specifically made sure I was the last bus back in the yard so they could log my exact mileage. I'm agonizing over what they're planning for my Monday route, all while feeling like I can't even move a muscle to get up and get a glass of water. \n\nSo, no, there hasn't been a break in the emptiness. I haven't had a normal energy day in a very long time. The heavy, blank feeling is just there every single second, completely layered right on top of the tracking and the radio stuff. They don't take turns, you know? It's just all happening at once, all the time, and I haven't had a single day where I felt like my old self.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period—lasting several days or more—where you felt unusually energetic, elated, or irritable, needed much less sleep than usual, or found yourself talking faster or taking on more risks than normal?\n\nPatient: No, I... I definitely haven't ever felt anything like that. Honestly, the idea of having extra energy or not needing sleep sounds like it belongs on another planet right now, but even before all this started, no. I've never been that kind of person. \n\nI've always been someone who absolutely needs my sleep. Even back when I was getting my associate's degree, I remember all these other students would be pulling all-nighters, drinking those massive energy drinks and staying up for two days straight to study for midterms, and I just physically couldn't do it. My brain would completely shut down by midnight. And then once I started working in transportation, you know, you really have to be alert. You're operating a massive vehicle with other people's safety in your hands, so you have to be rested. I've always been incredibly strict about my sleep schedule because of that. I'd always aim for a solid eight hours, sometimes nine if it was a particularly grueling week on the road. So the idea of just not needing sleep for days at a time and feeling elated... no, I've never experienced anything remotely like that. \n\nAnd as for taking risks or talking super fast, that's really not me either. Like I said, my whole professional life is built around minimizing risk. Before I even put the key in the ignition, I do a full pre-trip inspection. I check the tire pressure, I test the air brakes, I make sure every single mirror is perfectly aligned so I don't have any blind spots. I don't speed, I don't run yellow lights, I don't gamble or do anything reckless in my personal life either. I've always been a very careful, methodical person. Even when I'm explaining things, I think I take my time to make sure I'm getting all the details right, rather than just rushing through it and talking a mile a minute. So, no, I've never had a period like what you're describing. It was always just my normal, steady routine for years, and then... well, then this heavy, empty feeling started eight months ago, and I've just been stuck at the absolute bottom of the barrel ever since.\n\nDoctor: Before these last eight months, have you ever had a similar period in the past—lasting two weeks or more—where you felt this same kind of deep emptiness, low energy, and loss of interest in things?\n\nPatient: No... no, I really haven't. Before this whole thing started eight months ago with that temporary dispatch job, I was always... well, I was just a really steady person. Like I was saying about my sleep and my routine, I just kept things moving. \n\nI think back to when I was finishing my associate's degree, and then right after when I first got my commercial driver's license and started with the transit authority. I was always the one who was organizing things, you know? I'd be the one sending out the group texts to set up the softball practices, or I'd make sure everyone in the alto section for choir had their sheet music organized in the right binders before Sunday. I never had a time where I just didn't care. Not for two weeks, not even for two days. \n\nI mean, sure, everyone has a bad day here and there. Like if you catch a bad head cold, or if the weather is just miserable and rainy all week and you don't feel like going out to the grocery store. But this deep, heavy emptiness? This feeling like I'm made of wet cement and literally nothing in the world matters? That is completely new. It never, ever happened before eight months ago. \n\nThat's why it's so hard to explain to my friends, or really anyone who knew me before this year, because they would all tell you I was always the reliable one. I was the one with the energy to keep the schedule on track and make sure everyone was where they needed to be. So to just fall into this massive hole and not be able to get out, to not even want to look at my softball glove... it's just never been a part of my life before. It's strictly been these last eight months. Before that, my life was just completely normal and... I don't know, full? It felt full. Not hollow like this. So, no, I've never had a period like this in the past.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like I was saying about my sleep and my routine, I just kept things moving. \n\nI think back to when I was finishing my associate's degree, and then right after when I first got my commercial driver's license and started with the transit authority. I was always the one who was organizing things, you know? I'd be the one sending out the group texts to set up the softball practices, or I'd make sure everyone in the alto section for choir had their sheet music organized in the right binders before Sunday. I never had a time where I just didn't care. Not for two weeks, not even for two days. \n\nI mean, sure, everyone has a bad day here and there. Like if you catch a bad head cold, or if the weather is just miserable and rainy all week and you don't feel like going out to the grocery store. But this deep, heavy emptiness? This feeling like I'm made of wet cement and literally nothing in the world matters? That is completely new. It never, ever happened before eight months ago. \n\nThat's why it's so hard to explain to my friends, or really anyone who knew me before this year, because they would all tell you I was always the reliable one. I was the one with the energy to keep the schedule on track and make sure everyone was where they needed to be. So to just fall into this massive hole and not be able to get out, to not even want to look at my softball glove... it's just never been a part of my life before. It's strictly been these last eight months. Before that, my life was just completely normal and... I don't know, full? It felt full. Not hollow like this. So, no, I've never had a period like this in the past."

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, I mean, I'm here because... it's just, it's a lot that's been going on at work lately, and it's getting to the point where I can't just ignore it anymore. I drive a bus, you know, for the city, and lately—well, not just lately, it's been building up for a while now—dispatch and compliance, they've been tracking my route changes. And I know everyone says, \"Oh, they track all the buses, it's just GPS, it's just normal oversight,\" but it's not just that. It's the way they do it. It's through the computer system, obviously, but then they make these... these little comments on the radio. Just tiny things that sound like regular dispatch talk to anyone else, but I know what they mean. They're letting me know they're watching my specific movements. And it's not just management either. I can tell certain other drivers are reporting on me. Like, they'll pull up at a layover or pass me on a route, and there's this look, or they'll key their mic right after I do. It's a whole system they've got set up just to monitor me, and people keep telling me it's just standard procedure, but I know the difference between standard procedure and being targeted. I really do. It's exhausting trying to drive safely when you know everyone is coordinating against you like that.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, no, it's nothing like that. I want to be really clear about that, because I know how this all sounds, but I'm not... I'm not hearing things that aren't there. The things I'm talking about, the radio clicks, the dispatchers, the comments from other drivers—those are physical sounds coming out of a physical speaker in the cab. Anyone on my bus can hear them. If a passenger is sitting in the priority seating right behind my partition, they hear the exact same voice from compliance that I do. They just don't understand the context, you know? They just hear the static and think it's, 'Oh, Bus 42, check your headway,' or whatever standard phrase dispatch is using to mask it that day. \n\nBut as far as, like, voices whispering in my ear or sounds in an empty room that literally no one else can physically hear? No, absolutely not. I mean, my work environment is incredibly loud to begin with. You've got the engine, the air brakes hissing, the doors opening and closing every two blocks, people talking loudly on their phones... so there's a lot of noise all the time. But my hearing is perfectly fine. I actually have to get my hearing checked for my DOT physical—the Department of Transportation requires a full medical clearance every two years to keep your commercial driver's license—and I always pass the hearing test with flying colors. They put you in the little booth with the headphones and you press the button when you hear the beep, and I never miss one. \n\nSo I hear exactly what exists in the real world. I'm not making up phantom voices or hearing ghosts or anything like that. It's just the real, actual voices on the radio that are the problem, because of what they're coordinating and how they're tracking me. So, no, I don't hear things other people can't hear. They hear the exact same audio I do, they just don't know what it actually means.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\n  \"key\": \"duration\",\n  \"name\": \"Duration\",\n  \"description\": \"8 months\",\n  \"reason\": \"The doctor is asking about the onset and duration of the negative symptoms (emptiness, low motivation), which corresponds to the overall duration of the illness covered by the 'duration' item.\"\n}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYou know, it's funny you ask if they started at the same time, because I actually tried to map it all out in my head recently. And no, it didn't start two months ago with the radio stuff. It's actually been going on a lot longer than that. \n\nIf I'm being completely honest, this feeling... this heavy, empty feeling... it's been building for about eight months now. Yeah, it's been a solid eight months. It started right around the time management put me in this temporary dispatch lead role at the depot. One of the regular supervisors had to go on an extended medical leave—I think it was for a knee replacement or something, he was always complaining about the stairs up to the breakroom—so they pulled me off my regular driving route to fill in for him. At the time, I thought it was going to be a really good opportunity, you know? A chance to sit in the office, learn the back-end of the routing system, see how everything is managed from the inside instead of just being out on the road all day. \n\nBut it was just... I don't know, something about it just started draining the life out of me. Sitting in that room, staring at the screens, dealing with the schedules... that's when I first noticed I didn't want to go to softball practice anymore. I'd finish my shift in the dispatch center, and I wouldn't even be physically tired because I'd just been sitting in a chair all day, but I'd feel so incredibly hollow. I just couldn't bring myself to go socialize. And then, even after the temporary role ended and they put me back behind the wheel on my normal route—which honestly should have been a relief—the feeling never went away. It just kept getting heavier and heavier. \n\nSo the tracking, the computer updates, the specific comments on the radio... all of that got undeniably real and locked in two months ago. But this feeling of being completely unplugged from my own life, the pulling away from choir and my friends? That's been creeping up on me for eight months, ever since I took that temporary dispatch job. It's just been this slow, steady fade out, month after month, until I ended up where I am now, just sitting in my house not able to move.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. But this past Sunday, I woke up at 7:00 AM—which is my normal time because my body clock is just so permanently locked into the early depot shift—and I just lay there in bed for hours. I was staring up at the ceiling fan. The blades have this little wobble to them on the medium setting, and I was just watching it go round and round, feeling completely drained. Like my arms and legs were literally made of wet cement. I didn't care about breakfast, I didn't care about the daylight outside, I just felt that massive, hollow emptiness I was telling you about. \n\nAnd even then, while I'm lying there feeling totally flat and unable to even push the blankets off me, my brain is still completely locked onto what dispatch is doing. I'm lying there in the quiet, but I'm thinking about how they updated the routing software on Friday afternoon right before my shift ended, and how they specifically made sure I was the last bus back in the yard so they could log my exact mileage. I'm agonizing over what they're planning for my Monday route, all while feeling like I can't even move a muscle to get up and get a glass of water. \n\nSo, no, there hasn't been a break in the emptiness. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, I... I definitely haven't ever felt anything like that. Honestly, the idea of having extra energy or not needing sleep sounds like it belongs on another planet right now, but even before all this started, no. I've never been that kind of person. \n\nI've always been someone who absolutely needs my sleep. Even back when I was getting my associate's degree, I remember all these other students would be pulling all-nighters, drinking those massive energy drinks and staying up for two days straight to study for midterms, and I just physically couldn't do it. My brain would completely shut down by midnight. And then once I started working in transportation, you know, you really have to be alert. You're operating a massive vehicle with other people's safety in your hands, so you have to be rested. I've always been incredibly strict about my sleep schedule because of that. I'd always aim for a solid eight hours, sometimes nine if it was a particularly grueling week on the road. So the idea of just not needing sleep for days at a time and feeling elated... no, I've never experienced anything remotely like that. \n\nAnd as for taking risks or talking super fast, that's really not me either. Like I said, my whole professional life is built around minimizing risk. Before I even put the key in the ignition, I do a full pre-trip inspection. I check the tire pressure, I test the air brakes, I make sure every single mirror is perfectly aligned so I don't have any blind spots. I don't speed, I don't run yellow lights, I don't gamble or do anything reckless in my personal life either. I've always been a very careful, methodical person. Even when I'm explaining things, I think I take my time to make sure I'm getting all the details right, rather than just rushing through it and talking a mile a minute. So, no, I've never had a period like what you're describing. It was always just my normal, steady routine for years, and then... well, then this heavy, empty feeling started eight months ago, and I've just been stuck at the absolute bottom of the barrel ever since.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.
